A grown-up-sized long-sleeved, ankle-length, henley-placket onesie, with no hood and a plain collar, with simple wrist cuffs and no attached gloves, and footed with built-in soles, in brushed fleece, a real plain stretch suit and not a real garment, covered edge to edge in a lifelike full-coverage print of the cloth with natural fabric texture and shading, designed to make the onesie look like the outfit of an orchestra conductor. The whole suit is the outfit, front, back, arms and legs (and any hood, gloves or feet) are printed as the matching parts of it, with the seams and the zip hidden in the pattern. The print shows a tailcoat with a white bow tie, striped trousers, polished shoes printed on the feet, and the sleeves end in plain cuffs with no hands printed. The back of the suit shows the matching back of the outfit, printed edge to edge, from the collar or hood to the ankles or feet. No background colour or plain jersey shows around the print, on the shoulders, sleeves or legs. Every sleeve and every leg of the suit is a tube covered completely, all the way round, in the outfit's cloth for that limb (sleeve cloth, trouser, tights or leg fabric), so no grey, blue or coloured background shows on the arms, legs, sides or back. Where the outfit leaves arms or legs bare, the suit itself is cut sleeveless, short-sleeved or short-legged at that point, so the wearer's own limbs show; no printed arm, leg, hand, foot or skin appears on the suit's fabric. Every part of the shirt, shoulders and sleeves included, shows the outfit's own colours and cloth texture in one uniform print. The outfit's cloth runs unbroken from the collar band across both shoulders and down both sleeves to their hems, so every part of the suit is outfit cloth. The outline is exactly a onesie's, nothing sticks out past the suit's edges, and no coat tails, skirts, capes, extra sleeves or loose layers hang outside it. The outfit is stretched to the suit's own straight-sided shape and fills it completely, so it suits any body size. The suit is solid and opaque and nothing is real but the jersey, textures and sheens are printed pictures only. It is not a drawing of a person standing in the middle of the suit, no body, no head, no limbs and no figure are pictured, only the outfit's cloth, trims and fastenings spread over the whole suit. The printed top of striped trousers reaching the hem and ending there. The onesie is a flat-printed illusion, the outfit appears on a plain stretch jersey suit with no real collars, belts or pockets. The suit is the whole orchestra conductor outfit, not just a top, with a tailcoat with a white bow tie, striped trousers on the legs, polished shoes (as the feet), a baton sewn or attached to the suit. It is shown in full, from the neckline down to the ankle cuffs. Sewn-on novelty extras, kept to the edges so the print stays unbroken, with a fuzzy collar edge, a large metallic zipper with a charm pull and soft terry-loop cuff trim. It is a bright, playful novelty onesie that is fun to touch.
A grown-up-sized long-sleeved, full-length-legged, hooded pull-on onesie, with a big attached hood, with built-in gloves at the sleeve ends, and with sewn-in bootie feet, in French terry, a real plain stretch suit and not a real garment, covered edge to edge in a retro screen-printed illustration with limited colours, designed to make the onesie look like the outfit of a dance instructor. No blank jersey shows, the printed outfit runs from the neckline down the arms and legs to the cuffs, and over the hood if there is one. The print shows a fitted top, trousers, with a plain hood printed in the outfit's colours, dance shoes printed on the feet, and printed gloves on the attached gloves at the sleeve ends. Front and back are both fully printed, the back showing how the outfit looks from behind. The outfit's own cloth reaches every seam, cuff and hem with no border or backdrop. The print is applied to the suit's own pattern pieces, front, back, each sleeve and each leg are fully covered in the matching cloth of the outfit, with no leftover background colour between them. There are no printed arms, hands, fingers, legs or feet and no printed skin on the suit, except a small printed neckline detail where the outfit is open. Every part of the shirt, shoulders and sleeves included, shows the outfit's own colours and cloth texture in one uniform print. The outfit's cloth runs unbroken from the collar band across both shoulders and down both sleeves to their hems, so every part of the suit is outfit cloth. The silhouette is a plain one-piece suit, with every part of the outfit printed inside its shape and none beyond it. The uniform fills the whole straight-sided suit shape edge to edge, the same width at chest, waist and hem. The suit is solid and opaque and nothing is real but the jersey, textures and sheens are printed pictures only. No standing figure, torso, arms or legs are depicted; the suit's own shape is the body, and the print is only the outfit's fabric covering it from edge to edge. It is a trompe-l'oeil onesie, a plain one-piece suit pretending to be the outfit through printing alone. A full dance instructor costume, head to toe in a single suit, with a fitted top, trousers on the legs, dance shoes (as the feet). Nothing of the outfit is missing and the legs of the suit show the lower garment. Sewn-on novelty extras, kept to the edges so the print stays unbroken, with a ruffled neck frill, a fluffy pom-pom at the tip of the hood and soft terry-loop cuff trim. It is a bright, playful novelty onesie that is fun to touch.

The print shows a polka-dot jumpsuit with a huge bow tie, baggy trousers, with a curly wig printed on the hood, oversized shoes printed on the feet, and the sleeves end in plain cuffs with no hands printed. Front and back are both fully printed, the back showing how the outfit looks from behind. There is no separate background, the print is cut to the onesie's shape. Every sleeve and every leg of the suit is a tube covered completely, all the way round, in the outfit's cloth for that limb (sleeve cloth, trouser, tights or leg fabric), so no grey, blue or coloured background shows on the arms, legs, sides or back. There are no printed arms, hands, fingers, legs or feet and no printed skin on the suit, except a small printed neckline detail where the outfit is open. Every part of the shirt, shoulders and sleeves included, shows the outfit's own colours and cloth texture in one uniform print. The outfit's cloth runs unbroken from the collar band across both shoulders and down both sleeves to their hems, so every part of the suit is outfit cloth. The silhouette is a plain one-piece suit, with every part of the outfit printed inside its shape and none beyond it. The uniform fills the whole straight-sided suit shape edge to edge, the same width at chest, waist and hem. Everything is flat ink on opaque stretch jersey, lace, fur, metal, leather and sequins are printed patterns, never real raised or see-through material. It is not a drawing of a person standing in the middle of the suit, no body, no head, no limbs and no figure are pictured, only the outfit's cloth, trims and fastenings spread over the whole suit. It is a trompe-l'oeil onesie, a plain one-piece suit pretending to be the outfit through printing alone. A full clown costume, head to toe in a single suit, with a polka-dot jumpsuit with a huge bow tie, baggy trousers on the legs, a curly wig (as the hood), oversized shoes (as the feet), a squeaky horn sewn or attached to the suit. Nothing of the outfit is missing and the legs of the suit show the lower garment. Sewn-on novelty extras, kept to the edges so the print stays unbroken, glittery elastic cuffs, a fleece-lined hood interior in a bright colour and a ruffled neck frill. It is a bright, playful novelty onesie that is fun to touch.

The suit is solid and opaque and nothing is real but the jersey, textures and sheens are printed pictures only.
